\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}{{{}{}}}{}{{{}{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 19.69 -9.84 C 29.53 -9.84 29.53 59.06 39.37 59.06}{fill:none} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\pgfinnerlinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 19.69 -9.84 C 29.53 -9.84 29.53 59.06 39.37 59.06}{fill:none} }\lxSVG@closescope \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{33.40848pt}{40.81256pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{73}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}{}}}{{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-8.85431pt}{40.81256pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{73}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{matrix}\qquad\quad\hbox{for $j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}$.}}

Proposition 3.2
.

For
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
,

\displaystyle Y_{i}Y_{j}=Y_{j}Y_{i},\qquad T_{\pi}^{n}=Y_{1}\cdots Y_{n}\quad\hbox{and}\quad Y_{1}Y_{n}^{-1}=T_{0}T_{n-1}\cdots T_{1}\cdots T_{n-1}.

(3.8)

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{l}T_{i}Y_{i}=Y_{i+1}T_{i}+(t^{\frac{1}{2}}-t^{-\frac{1}{2}})Y_{i},\\
T_{i}Y_{i+1}=Y_{i}T_{i}-(t^{\frac{1}{2}}-t^{-\frac{1}{2}})Y_{i},\end{array}\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad T_{i}Y_{j}=Y_{j}T_{i}\quad\hbox{if $j\not\in\{i,i+1\}$.}

Proof.

The pictorial representation provides an easy check of the relations in (
3.8
) and the second relation in (
3.2
).

The relations
Y_{i+1}=T^{-1}_{i}Y_{i}T^{-1}_{i}
and
T_{i}^{2}=(t^{\frac{1}{2}}-t^{-\frac{1}{2}})T_{i}+1

and
T_{i}-T_{i}^{-1}=t^{\frac{1}{2}}-t^{-\frac{1}{2}}
give the other two relations:

\displaystyle T_{i}Y_{i}

\displaystyle=((t^{\frac{1}{2}}-t^{-\frac{1}{2}})+T^{-1}_{i})Y_{i}=Y_{i+1}T_{i}+(t^{\frac{1}{2}}-t^{-\frac{1}{2}})Y_{i},

\displaystyle T_{i}Y_{i+1}

\displaystyle=Y_{i}T^{-1}_{i}=Y_{i}(T_{i}-(t^{\frac{1}{2}}-t^{-\frac{1}{2}}))=Y_{i}T_{i}-(t^{\frac{1}{2}}-t^{-\frac{1}{2}})Y_{i}.

∎

3.3
Creation operators

Next, we look at the relation between the creation operators (or intertwiners) and the Cherednick-Dunkl operators.

Proposition 3.3
.

For
i\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
and
j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
with
j\not\in\{i,i+1\}
,

\tau^{\vee}_{i}Y_{i}=Y_{i+1}\tau^{\vee}_{i},\qquad\tau_{i}^{\vee}Y_{i+1}=Y_{i}\tau^{\vee}_{i},\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad\tau^{\vee}_{i}Y_{j}=Y_{j}\tau^{\vee}_{i}.

(3.11)

Moreover, for
j\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
,

\tau^{\vee}_{\pi}Y_{j}=Y_{j+1}\tau^{\vee}_{\pi}\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad\tau^{\vee}_{\pi}Y_{n}=q^{-1}Y_{1}\tau^{\vee}_{\pi}.

(3.12)

Proof.

The statements in (
3.11
) follow from the relations (
3.2
) and the fact that the
Y_{i}
’s all commute with each other.

For
i\in\{1,\ldots,n-2\}
,

\displaystyle T^{\vee}_{\pi}T_{i}

\displaystyle=X_{1}T_{1}\cdots T_{n-1}T_{i}=X_{1}T_{1}\cdots T_{i}T_{i+1}T_{i}T_{i+2}\cdots T_{n-1}=X_{1}T_{1}\cdots T_{i-1}T_{i+1}T_{i}T_{i+1}T_{i+2}\cdots T_{n-1}

\displaystyle=X_{1}T_{i+1}T_{1}\cdots T_{i-1}T_{i}T_{i+1}\cdots T_{n-1}=T_{i+1}X_{1}T_{1}\cdots T_{n-1}=T_{i+1}T^{\vee}_{\pi}.

Using these relations and the relations from (
3.7
),

\displaystyle\tau^{\vee}_{\pi}Y_{1}

\displaystyle=T_{\pi}^{\vee}T_{\pi}T_{n-1}\cdots T_{1}=T_{1}^{-1}T_{\pi}T_{\pi}^{\vee}T_{n-2}\cdots T_{1}=T_{1}^{-1}T_{\pi}T_{n-1}\cdots T_{2}T_{\pi}^{\vee}

\displaystyle=T_{1}^{-1}T_{\pi}T_{n-1}\cdots T_{2}T_{1}T_{1}^{-1}T_{\pi}^{\vee}=T_{1}^{-1}Y_{1}T_{1}^{-1}T_{\pi}^{\vee}=Y_{2}T_{\pi}^{\vee}=Y_{2}\tau^{\vee}_{\pi},\quad\hbox{and}

\displaystyle\tau^{\vee}_{\pi}Y_{n}

\displaystyle=T_{\pi}^{\vee}T^{-1}_{n-1}\cdots T_{1}^{-1}Y_{1}T_{1}^{-1}\cdots T_{n-1}^{-1}

\displaystyle=T_{\pi}^{\vee}T^{-1}_{n-1}\cdots T_{1}^{-1}T_{\pi}T_{n-1}\cdots T_{1}T_{1}^{-1}\cdots T_{n-1}^{-1}=T_{\pi}^{\vee}T^{-1}_{n-1}\cdots T_{1}^{-1}T_{\pi}(T_{\pi}^{\vee})^{-1}T_{\pi}^{\vee}

\displaystyle=T_{\pi}^{\vee}q(T_{\pi}^{\vee})^{-1}T_{\pi}T_{n-1}\cdots T_{1}T_{\pi}^{\vee}=qY_{1}T_{\pi}^{\vee}=qY_{1}\tau^{\vee}_{\pi}.

Now, for
i\in\{2,\ldots,n-1\}
, we use induction, so that

\tau^{\vee}_{\pi}Y_{i}=T_{\pi}^{\vee}T_{i-1}^{-1}\cdots T_{1}^{-1}Y_{1}T_{1}^{-1}\cdots T_{i-1}^{-1}=T_{i}^{-1}\cdots T_{2}^{-1}Y_{2}T_{2}^{-1}\cdots T_{i}^{-1}T_{\pi}^{\vee}=Y_{i+1}T_{\pi}^{\vee}=Y_{i+1}\tau^{\vee}_{\pi}.

∎

3.4
XY-parallelism

In this section, we highlight the parallelism between the
X_{i}
operators and the
Y_{i}
operators.
To do so, we define the normalized intertwiners, which are defined in terms of the
c
-functions.
For
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}
with
i\neq j
, define the
c
-fuctions

c^{X}_{ij}=\frac{t^{-\frac{1}{2}}-t^{\frac{1}{2}}X_{i}X_{j}^{-1}}{1-X_{i}X_{j}^{-1}}\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad c^{Y}_{ij}=\frac{t^{-\frac{1}{2}}-t^{\frac{1}{2}}Y_{i}Y_{j}^{-1}}{1-Y_{i}Y_{j}^{-1}}.

For
i\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
define the normalized intertwiners
\eta_{s_{i}}
and
\xi_{s_{i}}
by

\eta_{s_{i}}=\frac{1}{c^{Y}_{i+1,i}}(C_{s_{i}}-c^{Y}_{i,i+1})\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad\xi_{s_{i}}=\frac{1}{c^{X}_{i,i+1}}(C_{s_{i}}-c^{X}_{i+1,i}).

Proposition 3.4
.

For
i,j,k\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
with
k\not\in\{j\pm 1\}
,

\displaystyle\eta_{s_{i}}\eta_{s_{i+1}}\eta_{s_{i}}

\displaystyle=\eta_{s_{i}}\eta_{s_{i+1}}\eta_{s_{i}},

\displaystyle\eta_{s_{j}}\eta_{s_{k}}

\displaystyle=\eta_{s_{k}}\eta_{s_{j}},

\displaystyle\eta_{s_{i}}C_{s_{i}}

\displaystyle=C_{s_{i}},

(3.13)

\displaystyle\xi_{s_{i}}\xi_{s_{i+1}}\xi_{s_{i}}

\displaystyle=\xi_{s_{i}}\xi_{s_{i+1}}\xi_{s_{i}},

\displaystyle\xi_{s_{j}}\xi_{s_{k}}

\displaystyle=\xi_{s_{k}}\xi_{s_{j}},

\displaystyle\xi_{s_{i}}C_{s_{i}}

\displaystyle=C_{s_{i}}.

Moreover, for
i\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
and
j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
,

\begin{array}[]{l}\eta_{s_{i}}Y_{i}=Y_{i+1}\eta_{s_{i}},\\
\eta_{s_{i}}Y_{i+1}=Y_{i}\eta_{s_{i}},\\
\eta_{s_{i}}Y_{j}=Y_{j}\eta_{s_{i}},\quad\hbox{if $j\not\in\{i,i+1\}$,}\end{array}\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad\begin{array}[]{l}\xi_{s_{i}}X_{i}=X_{i+1}\xi_{s_{i}},\\
\xi_{s_{i}}X_{i+1}=X_{i}\xi_{s_{i}},\\
\xi_{s_{i}}X_{j}=X_{j}\xi_{s_{i}},\quad\hbox{if $j\not\in\{i,i+1\}$.}\end{array}

(3.14)

Proof.

The first set of relations in (
3.14
) follow from the relations in (
3.11
).

Using the relations

C_{s_{i}}^{2}=(t^{\frac{1}{2}}+t^{-\frac{1}{2}})C_{s_{i}},\qquad C_{s_{i}}=\tau^{\vee}_{i}+c^{Y}_{i,i+1},\qquad c^{Y}_{i,i+1}+c^{Y}_{i+1,i}=t^{\frac{1}{2}}+t^{-\frac{1}{2}},

and the relations in (
3.11
) gives

(\tau^{\vee}_{i})^{2}=c^{Y}_{i,i+1}c^{Y}_{i+1,i},\qquad(\eta_{s_{i}}+1)c_{i,i+1}^{Y}=C_{s_{i}}\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad\eta_{s_{i}}C_{s_{i}}=C_{s_{i}}.

(3.15)

A direct computation together with the relations in (
3.11
) produces

\displaystyle C_{s_{1}s_{2}s_{1}}

\displaystyle=C_{s_{1}}C_{s_{2}}C_{s_{1}}-C_{s_{1}}=C_{s_{2}}C_{s_{1}}C_{s_{2}}-C_{s_{2}}

\displaystyle=T_{1}T_{2}T_{1}+t^{-\frac{1}{2}}T_{1}T_{2}+t^{-\frac{1}{2}}T_{2}T_{1}+t^{-\frac{2}{2}}T_{1}+t^{-\frac{2}{2}}T_{2}+t^{-\frac{3}{2}},

\displaystyle=T_{2}T_{1}T_{2}+t^{-\frac{1}{2}}T_{1}T_{2}+t^{-\frac{1}{2}}T_{2}T_{1}+t^{-\frac{2}{2}}T_{1}+t^{-\frac{2}{2}}T_{2}+t^{-\frac{3}{2}},

\displaystyle=(\eta_{s_{1}}\eta_{s_{2}}\eta_{s_{1}}+\eta_{s_{1}}\eta_{s_{2}}+\eta_{s_{2}}\eta_{s_{1}}+\eta_{s_{2}}+\eta_{s_{1}}+1)c^{Y}_{12}c^{Y}_{13}c^{Y}_{23}

\displaystyle=(\eta_{s_{2}}\eta_{s_{1}}\eta_{s_{2}}+\eta_{s_{1}}\eta_{s_{2}}+\eta_{s_{2}}\eta_{s_{1}}+\eta_{s_{2}}+\eta_{s_{1}}+1)c^{Y}_{12}c^{Y}_{13}c^{Y}_{23}.

(3.16)

This establishes that
\eta_{s_{1}}\eta_{s_{2}}\eta_{s_{1}}=\eta_{s_{2}}\eta_{s_{1}}\eta_{s_{2}}
is
equivalent to
T_{1}T_{2}T_{1}=T_{2}T_{1}T_{2}
which is equivalent to

C_{s_{1}}C_{s_{2}}C_{s_{1}}-C_{s_{1}}=C_{s_{2}}C_{s_{1}}C_{s_{2}}-C_{s_{2}}
.

The relations for
\xi_{s_{i}}
are
the same as the relations for
\eta_{s_{i}}
because the relations in (
3.6
) are the same as the relations (
3.2
) except for a replacing

Y_{i}
with
X_{i+1}
and replacing
Y_{i+1}
with
X_{i}
. Thus, the same proof also works for the relations for
\xi_{s_{i}}
.
∎

The XY-parallelism in this section is the core of the duality in double affine Artin groups and
double affine Hecke algebras (see
[
Mac03
, §3.5]
).

Remark 3.5
.

The reader should be warned to be careful with denominators when working with the normalized interwiners.
When using the relations in Proposition
3.4

it is wise to always write any denominators as the leftmost factors so that
when acting on (left)
\tilde{H}
-modules the expressions are 0 as appropriate.
As a concrete example, from the first identity
in (
3.15
) it follows that

\eta_{s_{i}}^{2}=\frac{1}{c_{i,i+1}^{Y}c_{i+1,i}^{Y}}c^{Y}_{i,i+1}c_{i+1,i}^{Y}\,,\qquad\hbox{which \emph{does not always} reduce to}\quad\eta_{s_{i}}^{2}=1.

(3.17)

Even on the polynomial representation considered in Section
4
,
the operator
\eta_{s_{i}}
can act by
0
on some vectors (for example
\eta_{s_{i}}\cdot 1=0
).
However the identity for
\eta_{s_{i}}^{2}
in (
3.17
) is still valid since
\eta_{s_{i}}^{2}\cdot 1=0
and

\frac{1}{c_{i,i+1}^{Y}c_{i+1,i}^{Y}}c^{Y}_{i,i+1}c_{i+1,i}^{Y}\cdot 1=0\quad\hbox{(because $c_{i,i+1}^{Y}\cdot 1=0$),}

In the language of localizations of rings, the elements
\eta_{s_{i}}
and
\eta_{w}
(defined in (
3.18
))
do not live in the double affine Hecke algebra
\widetilde{H}
proper but in a localization at the multiplicative set generated
by the elements
(1-Y_{i}Y^{-1}_{j})
and
(1-tY_{i}Y^{-1}_{j})
.

3.5
Symmetrizers

Given
w\in S_{n}
with reduced word
w=s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{\ell}}
, we define

\xi_{w}=\xi_{s_{i_{1}}}\cdots\xi_{s_{i_{\ell}}},\qquad\eta_{w}=\eta_{s_{i_{1}}}\cdots\eta_{s_{i_{\ell}}},\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad T_{w}=T_{s_{i_{1}}}\cdots T_{s_{i_{\ell}}},

(3.18)

together with the following symmetrizers

X
-symmetrizer

\displaystyle p^{X}_{0}=\sum_{w\in S_{n}}\xi_{w}\ ,

X
-antisymmetrizer

\displaystyle e^{X}_{0}=\sum_{w\in S_{n}}(-1)^{\ell(w)-\ell(w_{0})}\xi_{w},

Y
-symmetrizer

\displaystyle p_{0}^{Y}=\sum_{w\in S_{n}}\eta_{w}\ ,

Y
-antisymmetrizer

\displaystyle e_{0}^{Y}=\sum_{w\in S_{n}}(-1)^{\ell(w)-\ell(w_{0})}\eta_{w}.

The
bosonic symmetrizer
and the
fermionic symmetrizer
are defined by

\mathbf{1}_{0}=\sum_{w\in S_{n}}t^{\frac{1}{2}(\ell(w)-\ell(w_{0}))}T_{w}\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad\varepsilon_{0}=\sum_{w\in S_{n}}(-t^{-\frac{1}{2}})^{\ell(w)-\ell(w_{0})}T_{w}.

(3.19)

The bosonic symmetrizer
\mathbf{1}_{0}
is a
t
-analogue of
p^{X}_{0}
and
p^{Y}_{0}

and the fermionic symmetrizer
\varepsilon_{0}
is a
t
-analogue of

e^{X}_{0}
and
e^{Y}_{0}
.
Using the relation
T_{i}^{2}=(t^{\frac{1}{2}}-t^{-\frac{1}{2}})T_{i}+1
and the last relation in (
3.15
)
gives

T_{i}\mathbf{1}_{0}=t^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathbf{1}_{0},\qquad C_{s_{i}}\mathbf{1}_{0}=(t^{\frac{1}{2}}+t^{-\frac{1}{2}})\mathbf{1}_{0}\quad\hbox{and}\qquad\eta_{s_{i}}\mathbf{1}_{0}=\mathbf{1}_{0},

since
\eta_{s_{i}}\mathbf{1}_{0}=\eta_{s_{i}}(t^{\frac{1}{2}}+t^{-\frac{1}{2}})^{-1}C_{s_{i}}\mathbf{1}_{0}=(t^{\frac{1}{2}}+t^{-\frac{1}{2}})^{-1}C_{s_{i}}\mathbf{1}_{0}=\mathbf{1}_{0}
.

Let us denote by
w_{0}
the longest element in
S_{n}
. That is,

w_{0}(i)=n-i+1
, for
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
. It has maximal length,
\ell(w_{0})=\frac{n(n-1)}{2}=\binom{n}{2}
, since its set of inversions is

\mathrm{Inv}(w_{0})=\{(i,j)\ |\ \hbox{$i,j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}$ and $i<j$}\}.

Then, we define

c_{w_{0}}^{X}=\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}c_{ij}^{X}=\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\frac{t^{-\frac{1}{2}}-t^{\frac{1}{2}}X_{i}X_{j}^{-1}}{1-X_{i}X_{j}^{-1}}\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad c_{w_{0}}^{X^{-1}}=\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\frac{t^{-\frac{1}{2}}-t^{\frac{1}{2}}X^{-1}_{i}X_{j}}{1-X^{-1}_{i}X_{j}}.

The following result rewrites the bosonic and fermionic symmetrizers in terms of the
X
-symmetrizers and the
Y
-symmetrizers.

Proposition 3.6
.

\mathbf{1}_{0}=p_{0}^{X}c_{w_{0}}^{X^{-1}}=p_{0}^{Y}c_{w_{0}}^{Y}\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad\varepsilon_{0}=c_{w_{0}}^{X}e_{0}^{X}=c_{w_{0}}^{Y^{-1}}e_{0}^{Y}.

(3.20)

Proof.

Let
w\in S_{n}
with reduced word
w=s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{\ell}}
.
Using that
T_{i}=\xi_{s_{i}}c_{i,i+1}^{X^{-1}}+(t^{\frac{1}{2}}-c_{i,i+1}^{X})
and expanding the result, we have that

T_{w}=T_{i_{1}}\cdots T_{i_{\ell}}=T_{w}=\xi_{w}c_{w}^{X^{-1}}+\sum_{v<w}\xi_{v}b_{v}(X),\qquad\hbox{with $b_{v}(X)\in\mathbb{C}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})$.}

Thus there are

a_{v}(X)\in\mathbb{C}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})
such that

\mathbf{1}_{0}=\sum_{w\in S_{n}}t^{-\frac{1}{2}\ell(w)-\ell(w_{0})}T_{w}=\xi_{w_{0}}c_{w_{0}}^{X^{-1}}+\sum_{w<w_{0}}\xi_{v}a_{v}(X),

(3.21)

Since
p^{X}_{0}=\sum_{w\in S_{n}}\xi_{w}
and

\xi_{s_{i}}p^{X}_{0}c^{X^{-1}}_{w_{0}}=p^{X}_{0}c^{X^{-1}}_{w_{0}}
then

\displaystyle T_{i}(p^{X}_{0}c^{X^{-1}}_{w_{0}})

\displaystyle=\big(c^{X}_{i,i+1}\xi_{s_{i}}+(t^{\frac{1}{2}}-c^{X}_{i,i+1})\big)p^{X}_{0}c_{w_{0}}^{X^{-1}}

\displaystyle=\big(c^{X}_{i,i+1}+(t^{\frac{1}{2}}-c^{X}_{i,i+1})\big)p^{X}_{0}c_{w_{0}}^{X^{-1}}=t^{\frac{1}{2}}\big(p^{X}_{0}c_{w_{0}}^{X^{-1}}\big).

Now,
\mathbf{1}_{0}
is determined, up to multiplication by a constant, by the fact that

T_{i}\mathbf{1}_{0}=t^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathbf{1}_{0}
for
i\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
, and so
it follows from (
3.21
) that

\mathbf{1}_{0}=p^{X}_{0}c_{w_{0}}^{X^{-1}}
(see the example for
n=3
in (
3.16
),
where
\mathbf{1}_{0}=C_{s_{1}s_{2}s_{1}}
).
The other relation in (
3.20
) is proven similarly.
∎

3.5.1
Symmetrizers and stabilizers

Let
\lambda\in(\mathbb{Z}^{n})^{+}
.
The stabilizer of
\lambda
under the action of
S_{n}
is

W_{\lambda}=\{v\in S_{n}\ |\ v\lambda=\lambda\}
, and denote by
w_{\lambda}
its longest element.
Let
W^{\lambda}
be the set of minimal length representatives of the cosets in
S_{n}/W_{\lambda}
, and denote by
v_{\lambda}
its longest element. Then,

w_{0}=v_{\lambda}w_{\lambda}
with
\ell(w_{0})=\ell(v_{\lambda})+\ell(w_{\lambda})
.
Consider the following symmetrizers and antisymmetrizers for
W^{\lambda}
and
W_{\lambda}
.

\begin{array}[]{lclll}\displaystyle{p^{\lambda}_{X}=\sum_{u\in W^{\lambda}}\xi_{u}}&\hbox{and}&\displaystyle{p^{X}_{\lambda}=\sum_{v\in W_{\lambda}}\xi_{v}}&\hbox{so that}&p_{0}^{X}=p^{\lambda}_{X}p^{X}_{\lambda},\\
\displaystyle{e^{\lambda}_{X}=\sum_{u\in W^{\lambda}}\det(v_{\lambda}u)\xi_{u}}&\hbox{and}&\displaystyle{e^{X}_{\lambda}=\sum_{v\in W_{\lambda}}\det(w_{\lambda}v)\xi_{v}}&\hbox{so that}&e_{0}^{X}=e^{\lambda}_{X}e^{X}_{\lambda},\\
\displaystyle{p^{\lambda}_{Y}=\sum_{u\in W^{\lambda}}\eta_{u}}&\hbox{and}&\displaystyle{p^{Y}_{\lambda}=\sum_{v\in W_{\lambda}}\eta_{v},}&\hbox{so that}&p_{0}^{Y}=p^{\lambda}_{Y}p^{Y}_{\lambda},\\
\displaystyle{e^{\lambda}_{Y}=\sum_{u\in W^{\lambda}}\det(v_{\lambda}u)\eta_{u}}&\hbox{and}&\displaystyle{e^{Y}_{\lambda}=\sum_{v\in W_{\lambda}}\det(w_{\lambda}v)\eta_{v}}&\hbox{so that}&e_{0}^{Y}=e^{\lambda}_{Y}e^{Y}_{\lambda}.\\
\end{array}

(3.22)

We also define the
t
-analogues of the elements in (
3.22
):

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\displaystyle{\mathbf{1}^{\lambda}=\sum_{u\in W^{\lambda}}(t^{\frac{1}{2}})^{\ell(u)-\ell(v_{\lambda})}T_{u}}&\hbox{and}&\displaystyle{\mathbf{1}_{\lambda}=\sum_{v\in W_{\lambda}}(t^{\frac{1}{2}})^{(\ell(v)-\ell(w_{\lambda})}T_{v},}\\
\\
\displaystyle{\varepsilon^{\lambda}=\sum_{u\in W^{\lambda}}(-t^{-\frac{1}{2}})^{\ell(u)-\ell(v_{\lambda})}T_{u}}&\hbox{and}&\displaystyle{\varepsilon_{\lambda}=\sum_{v\in W_{\lambda}}(-t^{-\frac{1}{2}})^{\ell(v)-\ell(w_{\lambda})}T_{v}.}\end{array}

(3.23)

Then

\mathbf{1}_{0}=\mathbf{1}^{\lambda}\mathbf{1}_{\lambda}
and

\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon^{\lambda}\varepsilon_{\lambda}
.

For
\lambda\in(\mathbb{Z}^{n})^{+}
, consider the sets of inversions of the elements
w_{\lambda}
and
v_{\lambda}
.

\displaystyle\mathrm{Inv}(w_{\lambda})

\displaystyle=\{(i,j)\ |\ \hbox{$i<j$ and $\lambda_{i}=\lambda_{j}$ }\}\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad\mathrm{Inv}(v_{\lambda})=\{(i,j)\ |\ \hbox{$i<j$ and $\lambda_{i}>\lambda_{j}$ }\}.

Thus, the associated
c
-functions are defined by

c^{X^{-1}}_{w_{\lambda}}=\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n\atop\lambda_{i}=\lambda_{j}}c_{ij}^{X^{-1}}\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad c^{X^{-1}}_{v_{\lambda}}=\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n\atop\lambda_{i}>\lambda_{j}}c_{ij}^{X^{-1}}.

The following is a generalization of Proposition
3.6
.

Proposition 3.7
.

For
\lambda\in(\mathbb{Z}^{n})^{+}
,

\mathbf{1}_{0}=p^{\lambda}_{X}c_{v_{\lambda}}^{X^{-1}}\mathbf{1}_{\lambda}=p^{\lambda}_{Y}c_{v_{\lambda}}^{Y}\mathbf{1}_{\lambda}\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad\varepsilon_{0}=c_{v_{\lambda}}^{X}e^{\lambda}_{X}\varepsilon_{\lambda}=c_{v_{\lambda}}^{Y^{-1}}e^{\lambda}_{Y}\varepsilon_{\lambda}.

Proof.

If
u\in W_{\lambda}
and
\lambda_{i}>\lambda_{j}
then
\lambda_{u(i)}>\lambda_{u(j)}
. Therefore,

u\cdot\mathrm{Inv}(v_{\lambda})=\{(u(i),u(j))\ |\ \hbox{$i<j$ and $\lambda_{i}>\lambda_{j}$}\}=\mathrm{Inv}(v_{\lambda}),

and so,
w^{-1}_{\lambda}c^{X}_{v_{\lambda}}=uc^{X}_{v_{\lambda}}=c^{X}_{v_{\lambda}}

for
u\in W_{\lambda}
. This shows that

c^{X^{-1}}_{w_{0}}=c^{X^{-1}}_{v_{\lambda}w_{\lambda}}=(w^{-1}_{\lambda}c^{X^{-1}}_{v_{\lambda}})c^{X^{-1}}_{w_{\lambda}}=c^{X^{-1}}_{v_{\lambda}}c^{X^{-1}}_{w_{\lambda}}\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad p_{\lambda}^{X}c^{X^{-1}}_{v_{\lambda}}=c^{X^{-1}}_{v_{\lambda}}p^{X}_{\lambda}.

(3.24)

Replacing
S_{n}
by the group
W_{\lambda}
in the proof of Proposition
3.6
gives

\mathbf{1}_{\lambda}=p^{X}_{\lambda}c_{w_{\lambda}}^{X^{-1}}
.
Using the relations in (
3.24
) and the identity

\mathbf{1}_{\lambda}=p^{X}_{\lambda}c_{w_{\lambda}}^{X^{-1}}
gives

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{0}

\displaystyle=p^{X}_{0}c_{w_{0}}^{X^{-1}}=p^{\lambda}_{X}p^{X}_{\lambda}(w^{-1}_{\lambda}c_{v_{\lambda}}^{X^{-1}})c_{w_{\lambda}}^{X^{-1}}=p^{\lambda}_{X}c_{v_{\lambda}}^{X^{-1}}p^{X}_{\lambda}c_{w_{\lambda}}^{X^{-1}}=p^{\lambda}_{X}c_{v_{\lambda}}^{X^{-1}}\mathbf{1}_{\lambda}.

The proof for
\varepsilon_{0}
is similar.
∎

4
The action on polynomials

In this section we see the operators of the last section acting on polynomials.
Using this action we can reinterpret the constructions of the Macdonald polynomials
given in Section
2
as the result of operators acting on the initial polynomial 1.
These are the ‘creation formulas’ for Macdonald polynomials. The symmetrizer formulas from Proposition
3.6
and Proposition
3.7

are then used to set up and prove the Boson-Fermion correspondence in the
Macdonald polynomial setting and to provide explicit combinatorial formulas for the
expansion of bosonic and fermionic Macdonald polynomials in terms of the basis
of electronic Macdonald polynomials. These symmetrizer formulas are
also the key to product formulas for the Poincaré polynomials which arise as the
normalizing constants for the bosonic Macdonald polynomials
P_{\lambda}
.

We have structured this section so that the proofs are postponed to the last subsection.
This allows us to first focus on the results and the “big picture” structure that relates the
bosonic, fermionic, and electronic Macdonald polynomials and leaves the necessary computations
to the last subsection.

4.1
DAHA acts on polynomials

Recall the definition of the
divided difference operators

\partial_{i}\colon\mathbb{C}[X]\to\mathbb{C}[X]
, the
Hecke algebra operators

T_{i}\colon\mathbb{C}[X]\to\mathbb{C}[X]
and the
promotion operator

T_{\pi}\colon\mathbb{C}[X]\to\mathbb{C}[X]
by

\partial_{i}f=\frac{f-s_{i}f}{x_{i}-x_{i+1}},\qquad T_{i}=t^{-\frac{1}{2}}x_{i+1}\partial_{i}-t^{\frac{1}{2}}\partial_{i}x_{i+1}\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad T_{\pi}=s_{1}\cdots s_{n-1}y_{n}.

(4.1)

Letting

s_{0}=T_{\pi}s_{n-1}T_{\pi}^{-1}=y_{1}y^{-1}_{n}s_{n-1}\cdots s_{2}s_{1}s_{2}\cdots s_{n-1},

then

T_{\pi}s_{0}T_{\pi}^{-1}=s_{1},\qquad T_{\pi}X_{n}T^{-1}_{\pi}=q^{-1}X_{1},\qquad T_{\pi}s_{i}T^{-1}_{\pi}=s_{i+1},\quad\hbox{and}\quad T_{\pi}X_{i}T^{-1}_{\pi}=X_{i+1},

for
i\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
.

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
\widetilde{H}
be the double affine Hecke algebra
as defined by generators and relations in Section
3.1
.
The formulas (
4.1
) define an action of
\widetilde{H}

on
\mathbb{C}[X]
.

A way of deriving the formulas in (
4.1
) is to consider the induced representation

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}[X]\cong\mathrm{Ind}_{H_{Y}}^{\widetilde{H}}(\mathbf{1}_{Y})=\widetilde{H}\mathbf{1}_{Y}=\hbox{$\mathbb{C}$-span}\{X_{1}^{\mu_{1}}\cdots X_{n}^{\mu_{n}}\mathbf{1}_{Y}\ |\ \mu=(\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}\}

determined by

T_{\pi}\mathbf{1}_{Y}=\mathbf{1}_{Y}\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad T_{i}\mathbf{1}_{Y}=t^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathbf{1}_{Y},\qquad\text{ for }i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}.

(4.2)

Then the formulas in (
4.1
) are consequences of the relations in
(
3.4
) and (
3.5
). In other words, the map

\begin{matrix}\mathbb{C}[X]&\longrightarrow&\widetilde{H}\mathbf{1}_{Y}\\
x^{\mu}&\longmapsto&X^{\mu}\mathbf{1}_{Y}\end{matrix}\qquad\hbox{is a $\widetilde{H}$-module isomorphism.}

(4.3)

4.2
Creation formulas

Let
\mu=(\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{n}
.
The
minimal length permutation

v_{\mu}
such that
v_{\mu}\mu
is weakly increasing
is given by

v_{\mu}(r)=1+\#\{r^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,r-1\}\ |\ \mu_{r^{\prime}}\leq\mu_{r}\}+\#\{r^{\prime}\in\{r+1,\ldots,n\}\ |\ \mu_{r^{\prime}}<\mu_{r}\},

for
r\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
. A
box in
\mu
is a pair
(r,c)
with

r\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
and
c\in\{1,\ldots,\mu_{r}\}
.
If
b=(r,c)
is a box in
\mu
then define

u_{\mu}(r,c)=\#\{r^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,r-1\}\ |\ \mu_{r^{\prime}}\leq c-1\}+\#\{r^{\prime}\in\{r+1,\ldots,n\}\ |\ \mu_{r^{\prime}}<c-1\}.

Let us take the following creation formula for
E_{\mu}

from
[
GR21
, Proposition 5.5 and Proposition 2.2(a)]
, where a complete proof is included.

Theorem 4.2
.

Let
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{n}
. Letting

\tau^{\vee}_{u_{\mu}}=\prod_{(r,c)\in\mu}(\tau^{\vee}_{u_{\mu}(r,c)}\cdots\tau^{\vee}_{2}\tau^{\vee}_{1}\tau^{\vee}_{\pi}),\qquad\hbox{then}\qquad E_{\mu}=t^{-\frac{1}{2}\ell(v^{-1}_{\mu})}\tau^{\vee}_{u_{\mu}}1,

where
1
is the polynomial
1\in\mathbb{C}[X]
.

The creation formulas for
P_{\lambda}
and
A_{\lambda+\rho}
are given in terms of the bosonic and fermionic symmetrizers defined in (
3.19
).

Theorem 4.3
.

Let
\lambda\in\left(\mathbb{Z}^{n}\right)^{++}
, and recall that
\rho=(n-1,n-2,\ldots,1)
. Then

P_{\lambda}=\frac{t^{\frac{1}{2}\ell(w_{0})}}{W_{\lambda}(t)}\mathbf{1}_{0}E_{\lambda}\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad A_{\lambda+\rho}=t^{\frac{1}{2}\ell(w_{0})}\varepsilon_{0}E_{\lambda+\rho},

(4.4)

where
W_{\lambda}(t)
is a normalizing constant which makes the coefficient of
x^{\lambda}
in
P_{\lambda}
equal to
1
.

By (
3.20
), the formulas in (
4.4
) match the formulas in
(
2.1
) and (
2.2
).
The constant

W_{\lambda}(t)
is determined explicitly in Proposition
4.6
.

4.3
The Boson-Fermion correspondence

Define the following vector subspaces of
\mathbb{C}[X]

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}[X]^{S_{n}}

\displaystyle=\{f\in\mathbb{C}[X]\ |\ s_{i}f=f\ \text{ for all }i\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}\},

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}[X]^{\mathrm{det}}

\displaystyle=\{f\in\mathbb{C}[X]\ |\ s_{i}f=-f\text{ for all }i\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}\},

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}[X]^{\mathrm{Bos}}

\displaystyle=\{f\in\mathbb{C}[X]\ |\ T_{s_{i}}f=t^{\frac{1}{2}}f\text{ for all }i\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}\},

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}[X]^{\mathrm{Fer}}

\displaystyle=\{f\in\mathbb{C}[X]\ |\ T_{s_{i}}f=-t^{-\frac{1}{2}}f\text{ for all }i\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}\}.

The Boson-Fermion correspondence in Theorem
4.4
puts the formulas in (
4.4
) into a structural context.

Theorem 4.4
.

Let
\mathbf{1}_{0}
and
\varepsilon_{0}
be as defined in (
3.19
).
Then

\mathbf{1}_{0}\mathbb{C}[X]=\mathbb{C}[X]^{\mathrm{Bos}}=\mathbb{C}[X]^{S_{n}}\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad\varepsilon_{0}\mathbb{C}[X]=\mathbb{C}[X]^{\mathrm{Fer}}=A_{\rho}\mathbb{C}[X]^{S_{n}}.

Moreover, there are
\mathbb{C}[X]^{S_{n}}
-module isomorphisms

\begin{matrix}\mathbb{C}[X]^{S_{n}}&\to&\mathbb{C}[X]^{\mathrm{det}}\\
f&\mapsto&a_{\rho}f\end{matrix}\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad\begin{matrix}\mathbb{C}[X]^{\mathrm{Bos}}&\to&\mathbb{C}[X]^{\mathrm{Fer}}\\
f&\mapsto&A_{\rho}f\end{matrix}

(4.5)

where

a_{\rho}=\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}(x_{j}-x_{i})\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad A_{\rho}=\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}(x_{j}-tx_{i}),

(4.6)

4.4
The Poincaré polynomial
W_{0}(t)

The
Poincaré polynomial
for the symmetric group
S_{n}
is

W_{0}(t)=\sum_{w\in S_{n}}t^{\ell(w)}.

The following result writes the Poincaré polynomial in terms of the
c
-functions

c_{w_{0}}^{Y}
and

c_{w_{0}}^{X^{-1}}
.

Proposition 4.5
.

We have that

t^{-\frac{1}{2}\ell(w_{0})}W_{0}(t)=\mathrm{ev}^{t}_{0}(c_{w_{0}}^{Y})=\sum_{w\in S_{n}}wc_{w_{0}}^{X^{-1}}

(4.7)

and

W_{0}(t)=\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\frac{1-t^{j-i+1}}{1-t^{j-i}}=[n]!,\qquad\hbox{where}\qquad[n]!=\prod_{d=1}^{n}\frac{1-t^{d}}{1-t}.

By Proposition
4.5
,

W_{0}(t)=\sum_{w\in S_{n}}wt^{\frac{1}{2}\ell(w_{0})}c_{w_{0}}^{X^{-1}}=\sum_{w\in S_{n}}w\Big(\prod_{i<j}\frac{x_{i}-tx_{j}}{x_{i}-x_{j}}\Big).

This coincides with the definition of
W_{0}(t)
(see (
2.1
)) as the
appropriate constant that makes the coefficient of
1=x^{0}=x_{1}^{0}\cdots x_{n}^{0}
in

P_{0}=\frac{1}{W_{0}(t)}\sum_{w\in S_{n}}w\Big(\prod_{i<j}\frac{x_{i}-tx_{j}}{x_{i}-x_{j}}\Big).

The following result is the analog for the subgroup
W_{\lambda}
, and its proof is the same as for
Proposition
4.5
,
except restricted to the subgroup
W_{\lambda}
. It provides explicit formulas for the
normalizing factor
W_{\lambda}(t)
which makes the coefficient of
x^{\lambda}
in the
bosonic Macdonald polynomial
P_{\lambda}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n};q,t)
equal to
1
.

Proposition 4.6
.

Let
\lambda\in\left(\mathbb{Z}^{n}\right)^{+}
. Let

W_{\lambda}=\{v\in S_{n}\ |\ v\lambda=\lambda\}
be the stabilizer of
\lambda
in
S_{n}
and

W_{\lambda}(t)
the length generating
function for
W_{\lambda}
. Then

t^{-\frac{1}{2}\ell(w_{\lambda})}W_{\lambda}(t)=\mathrm{ev}^{t}_{0}(c_{w_{\lambda}}^{Y})=\sum_{w\in W_{\lambda}}wc_{w_{\lambda}}^{X^{-1}},

where
w_{\lambda}
is the longest element of
W_{\lambda}
. Alternatively,

W_{\lambda}(t)=\sum_{w\in W_{\lambda}}w\Big(\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n\atop\lambda_{i}=\lambda_{j}}\frac{1-tx^{-1}_{i}x_{j}}{1-x^{-1}_{i}x_{j}}\Big)=\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n\atop\lambda_{i}=\lambda_{j}}\frac{1-t^{j-i+1}}{1-t^{j-i}}=\prod_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}[m_{i}]!,

where
m_{i}=\#\{k\in\{1,\ldots,n\}\ |\ \lambda_{k}=i\}
.

Example 4.1
.

For
\lambda=(5,5,3,2,2,2,2,2,-1,-1,-1)
, we have that
m_{5}=2
,
m_{3}=1
,
m_{2}=5
,

m_{-1}=3
. By Proposition
4.6
,

W_{\lambda}=S_{5}\times S_{1}\times S_{5}\times S_{3}\subseteq S_{11}\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad W_{\lambda}(t)=[2]!\cdot[1]!\cdot[5]!\cdot[3]!.

4.5
H_{Y}
-decomposition of the polynomial representation

Let
H_{Y}
be the algebra generated by the operators
T_{1},\ldots,T_{n-1}

and
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n}
(so that
H_{Y}
is an affine Hecke algebra).
As
H_{Y}
-modules

\mathbb{C}[X]=\bigoplus_{\lambda}\mathbb{C}[X]^{\lambda}\qquad\hbox{where}\qquad\mathbb{C}[X]^{\lambda}=\hbox{span}\{E_{\mu}\ |\ \mu\in S_{n}\lambda\},

where the direct sum is over decreasing
\lambda=(\lambda_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
, and

S_{n}\lambda
denotes the set of distinct rearrangements of
\lambda
.

A description of the action of
H_{Y}
on
\mathbb{C}[X]^{\lambda}
is given by the following.
Let
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
and
v_{\mu}\in S_{n}
be the minimal length permutation such that
v_{\mu}\mu
is weakly increasing. Fix
i\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
, and define

\begin{array}[]{l}a_{\mu}=q^{\mu_{i}-\mu_{i+1}}t^{v_{\mu}(i)-v_{\mu}(i+1)},\\
a_{s_{i}\mu}=q^{\mu_{i+1}-\mu_{i}}t^{v_{\mu}(i+1)-v_{\mu}(i)},\end{array}\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad D_{\mu}=\frac{(1-ta_{\mu})(1-ta_{s_{i}\mu})}{(1-a_{\mu})(1-a_{s_{i}\mu})}.

We have two cases depending on the relationship between
\mu_{i}
and
\mu_{i+1}
.

Case 1:

\mu_{i}>\mu_{i+1}
. Using the identity
E_{s_{i}\mu}=t^{\frac{1}{2}}\tau^{\vee}_{i}E_{\mu}
if
\mu_{i}>\mu_{i+1}
from (E2), the eigenvalue from Theorem
1.1
and the formulas in (
1.14
) for
\tau_{i}^{\vee}
,

\begin{array}[]{l}Y_{i}^{-1}Y_{i+1}E_{\mu}=a_{\mu}E_{\mu},\\
Y_{i}^{-1}Y_{i+1}E_{s_{i}\mu}=a_{s_{i}\mu}E_{s_{i}\mu},\end{array}\quad\begin{array}[]{l}t^{\frac{1}{2}}\tau^{\vee}_{i}E_{\mu}=E_{s_{i}\mu},\\
t^{\frac{1}{2}}\tau^{\vee}_{i}E_{s_{i}\mu}=D_{\mu}E_{\mu},\end{array}\quad\hbox{and}\quad\begin{array}[]{l}t^{\frac{1}{2}}T_{i}E_{\mu}=-\frac{1-t}{1-a_{\mu}}E_{\mu}+E_{s_{i}\mu},\\
t^{\frac{1}{2}}T_{i}E_{s_{i}\mu}=D_{\mu}E_{\mu}+\frac{1-t}{1-a_{s_{i}\mu}}E_{s_{i}\mu}.\end{array}

(4.8)

Case 2:
If
\mu_{i}=\mu_{i+1}
then

v_{\mu}(i+1)=v_{\mu}(i)+1
and
a_{\mu}=t^{-1}
, so that

Y_{i}^{-1}Y_{i+1}E_{\mu}=t^{-1}E_{\mu},\qquad(t^{\frac{1}{2}}\tau^{\vee}_{i})E_{\mu}=0,\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad(t^{\frac{1}{2}}T_{i})E_{\mu}=tE_{\mu}.

(4.9)

These formulas make explicit the action of
H_{Y}
on
\mathbb{C}[X]^{\lambda}

in the basis
\{E_{\mu}\ |\ \mu\in S_{n}\lambda\}
.

4.6
E-expansions

Let
\mathbb{C}(Y)
be the field of fractions of
\mathbb{C}[Y]
.
For
\mu=(\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
define homomorphisms

\mathrm{ev}^{t}_{\mu}\colon\mathbb{C}[Y]\to\mathbb{C}[Y]
by

\mathrm{ev}^{t}_{\mu}(Y_{i})=q^{-\mu_{i}}t^{-(v_{\mu}(i)-1)+\frac{1}{2}(n-1)},\qquad\hbox{for $i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}$.}

Extend
\mathrm{ev}^{t}_{\mu}
to those elements of the field
\mathbb{C}(Y)
for which the evaluated denominator
is not
0
.
We refer to these homomorphisms as
evaluations
.
Then Theorem
1.1
gives that

fE_{\mu}=\mathrm{ev}^{t}_{\mu}(f)E_{\mu},\qquad\text{ for }f\in\mathbb{C}[Y]\text{ and }\mu\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}.

(4.10)

The following result expressed the bosonic and fermionic Macdonald polynomials in terms of the electronic Macdonald polynomials using the evaluations and
c
-functions.

Proposition 4.7
.

Let
\lambda\in\left(\mathbb{Z}^{n}\right)^{+}
. Then

\displaystyle P_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\sum_{z\in W^{\lambda}}t^{\frac{1}{2}\ell(v_{\lambda}z)}\mathrm{ev}^{t}_{z\lambda}(c^{Y}_{v_{\lambda}z})E_{z\lambda}\qquad\hbox{and}

\displaystyle A_{\lambda+\rho}

\displaystyle=\sum_{z\in W_{0}}(-t^{\frac{1}{2}})^{\ell(w_{0}z)}\mathrm{ev}^{t}_{z(\lambda+\rho)}(c^{Y^{-1}}_{w_{0}z})E_{z(\lambda+\rho)}.

Alternatively,

\displaystyle P_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mu\in S_{n}\lambda}\Big(\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n\atop\mu_{i}>\mu_{j}}t\Big(\frac{1-q^{\mu_{i}-\mu_{j}}t^{v_{\mu}(i)-v_{\mu}(j)-1}}{1-q^{\mu_{i}-\mu_{j}}t^{v_{\mu}(i)-v_{\mu}(j)}}\Big)\Big)E_{\mu}\qquad\hbox{and}

\displaystyle A_{\lambda+\rho}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mu\in S_{n}(\lambda+\rho)}\Big(\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n\atop\mu_{i}>\mu_{j}}(-1)\Big(\frac{1-q^{\mu_{i}-\mu_{j}}t^{v_{\mu}(i)-v_{\mu}(j)+1}}{1-q^{\mu_{i}-\mu_{j}}t^{v_{\mu}(i)-v_{\mu}(j)}}\Big)\Big)E_{\mu}.

Example 4.2
.

For
n=2
and
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
,

\displaystyle P_{(m,0)}

\displaystyle=E_{(0,m)}+t^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathrm{ev}^{t}_{(m,0)}(c^{Y}_{12})E_{(m,0)}=E_{(0,m)}+t\Big(\frac{1-q^{m}}{1-q^{m}t}\Big)E_{(m,0)},

\displaystyle A_{(m,0)}

\displaystyle=E_{(0,m)}-t^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathrm{ev}^{t}_{(m,0)}(c^{Y}_{21})E_{(m,0)}=E_{(0,m)}-\frac{1-q^{m}t^{2}}{1-q^{m}t}E_{(m,0)}.

Note that these expressions relate to
c
-functions since

t\Big(\frac{1-q^{m}}{1-q^{m}t}\Big)=t^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\frac{t^{-\frac{1}{2}}-t^{\frac{1}{2}}q^{-m}t^{-1}}{1-q^{-m}t^{-1}}\Big)
.

For
n=3
,

P_{(1,0,0)}=E_{(0,0,1)}+t\Big(\frac{1-q}{1-qt}\Big)E_{(0,1,0)}+t^{2}\Big(\frac{1-q}{1-qt}\Big)\Big(\frac{1-qt}{1-qt^{2}}\Big)E_{(1,0,0)}.

For general
n
and
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, denote
\varepsilon_{i}=(0,\ldots,0,1,0,\ldots,0)
, that is the sequence of
length
n
with
1
in the
i
th spot and
0
elsewhere. Then

\displaystyle P_{(m,0,\ldots,0)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}t^{n-i}\Big(\frac{1-q^{m}}{1-q^{m}t}\Big)\Big(\frac{1-q^{m}t}{1-q^{m}t^{2}}\Big)\cdots\Big(\frac{1-q^{m}t^{n-i-1}}{1-q^{m}t^{n-i}}\Big)E_{m\varepsilon_{i}}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}t^{n-i}\Big(\frac{1-q^{m}}{1-q^{m}t^{n-i}}\Big)E_{m\varepsilon_{i}}.

Example 4.3
.

\displaystyle P_{(2,1,0)}

\displaystyle=E_{(0,1,2)}+t\Big(\frac{1-q}{1-qt}\Big)E_{(1,0,2)}+t\Big(\frac{1-q}{1-qt}\Big)E_{(0,2,1)}+t^{2}\Big(\frac{1-qt}{1-qt^{2}}\Big)\Big(\frac{1-q^{2}}{1-q^{2}t}\Big)E_{(2,0,1)}

\displaystyle\qquad+t^{2}\Big(\frac{1-qt}{1-qt^{2}}\Big)\Big(\frac{1-q^{2}}{1-q^{2}t}\Big)E_{(1,2,0)}+t^{3}\Big(\frac{1-q}{1-qt}\Big)\Big(\frac{1-q^{2}t}{1-q^{2}t^{2}}\Big)\Big(\frac{1-q}{1-qt}\Big)E_{(2,1,0)}.

4.7
Proofs

4.7.1
Proof that the action on polynomials is a representation of
\widetilde{H}

Theorem
4.1
.

Let
\widetilde{H}
be the double affine Hecke algebra
as defined by generators and relations in Section
3.1
.
The formulas (
4.1
) define an action of
\widetilde{H}

on
\mathbb{C}[X]
.

Proof.

Let
i\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
.
Since
y_{n}X_{i}=X_{i}y_{n}
and
s_{1}\cdots s_{n-1}X_{i}=X_{i+1}s_{1}\cdots s_{n-1}

then

T_{\pi}X_{i}=s_{1}\cdots s_{n-1}y_{n}X_{i}=X_{i+1}s_{1}\cdots s_{n-1}y_{n}=X_{i+1}T_{\pi}.

As operators on
\mathbb{C}[X]
,

T_{\pi}X_{n}=s_{1}\cdots s_{n-1}y_{n}X_{n}=s_{1}\cdots s_{n-1}q^{-1}X_{n}y_{n}=q^{-1}X_{1}s_{1}\cdots s_{n-1}y_{n}=q^{-1}X_{1}T_{\pi}=X_{n+1}T_{\pi}.

Recall that we define
s_{0}=T_{\pi}s_{n-1}T_{\pi}^{-1}
so that

\displaystyle s_{0}

\displaystyle=T_{\pi}s_{n-1}T_{\pi}^{-1}=(y_{1}s_{1}\cdots s_{n-1})s_{n-1}(s_{n-1}\cdots s_{1}y^{-1}_{1})=y_{1}s_{1}\cdots s_{n-2}s_{n-1}s_{n-2}\cdots s_{1}y^{-1}_{1}

\displaystyle=y_{1}y^{-1}_{n}s_{1}\cdots s_{n-2}s_{n-1}s_{n-2}\cdots s_{1}=y_{1}y^{-1}_{n}s_{n-1}\cdots s_{2}s_{1}s_{2}\cdots s_{n-1}.

Then

\displaystyle T_{\pi}s_{0}T_{\pi}^{-1}=(s_{1}\cdots s_{n-1}y_{n})(y_{1}y^{-1}_{n}s_{n-1}\cdots s_{2}s_{1}s_{2}\cdots s_{n-1})(s_{n-1}\cdots s_{1}y^{-1}_{1})=s_{1}y_{2}y_{1}y_{2}^{-1}y_{1}^{-1}=s_{1}.

Define
\partial_{0}=T_{\pi}\partial_{n-1}T_{\pi}^{-1}
, so that

\displaystyle\partial_{0}=T_{\pi}\partial_{n-1}T_{\pi}^{-1}=T_{\pi}\frac{1}{x_{n-1}-x_{n}}(1-s_{n-1})T_{\pi}^{-1}=\frac{1}{x_{n}-q^{-1}x_{1}}(1-s_{0}).

Then

\displaystyle T_{\pi}\partial_{0}T^{-1}_{\pi}=T_{\pi}\frac{1}{x_{n}-q^{-1}x_{1}}(1-s_{0})T^{-1}_{\pi}=\frac{1}{q^{-1}x_{1}-q^{-1}x_{2}}(1-s_{1})=q\partial_{1}.

Finally,

\displaystyle T_{0}

\displaystyle=T_{\pi}T_{n-1}T^{-1}_{\pi}=T_{\pi}(t^{-\frac{1}{2}}x_{n}\partial_{n-1}-t^{\frac{1}{2}}\partial_{n-1}x_{n})T^{-1}_{\pi}=t^{-\frac{1}{2}}q^{-1}x_{1}\partial_{0}-t^{\frac{1}{2}}\partial_{0}q^{-1}x_{n},

and

\displaystyle T_{\pi}T_{0}T^{-1}_{\pi}=T_{\pi}(t^{-\frac{1}{2}}q^{-1}x_{1}\partial_{0}-t^{\frac{1}{2}}\partial_{0}q^{-1}x_{1})T^{-1}_{\pi}=t^{-\frac{1}{2}}q^{-1}x_{2}q\partial_{1}-t^{\frac{1}{2}}q\partial_{1}q^{-1}x_{2}=T_{1}.

These computations show that the relations in (
3.5
) hold.

Let
i\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
. Then, as operators on
\mathbb{C}[X]
,

\displaystyle\xi_{s_{i}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{c^{X}_{i,i+1}}(C_{s_{i}}-c^{X}_{i+1,i})=\frac{1}{c^{X}_{i,i+1}}(T_{i}+t^{-\frac{1}{2}}-c^{X}_{i+1,i})=\frac{1}{c^{X}_{i,i+1}}(t^{-\frac{1}{2}}x_{i+1}\partial_{i}-t^{\frac{1}{2}}\partial_{i}x_{i+1}+t^{-\frac{1}{2}}-c^{X}_{i+1,i})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{c^{X}_{i,i+1}}\Big(t^{-\frac{1}{2}}\frac{x_{i+1}}{x_{i}-x_{i+1}}(1-s_{i})-t^{\frac{1}{2}}\frac{1}{x_{i}-x_{i+1}}(1-s_{i})x_{i+1}+\frac{t^{-\frac{1}{2}}x_{i}-t^{-\frac{1}{2}}x_{i+1}}{x_{i}-x_{i+1}}-\frac{t^{-\frac{1}{2}}x_{i}-t^{\frac{1}{2}}x_{i+1}}{x_{i}-x_{i+1}}\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{c^{X}_{i,i+1}}\Big(\frac{t^{-\frac{1}{2}}x_{i+1}-t^{\frac{1}{2}}x_{i+1}+t^{-\frac{1}{2}}x_{i}-t^{-\frac{1}{2}}x_{i+1}-t^{-\frac{1}{2}}x_{i}+t^{\frac{1}{2}}x_{i+1}}{x_{i}-x_{i+1}}+\frac{-t^{-\frac{1}{2}}x_{i+1}+t^{\frac{1}{2}}x_{i}}{x_{i}-x_{i+1}}s_{i}\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{c^{X}_{i,i+1}}\Big(0+\frac{t^{-\frac{1}{2}}-t^{\frac{1}{2}}x_{i}x^{-1}_{i+1}}{1-x_{i}x^{-1}_{i+1}}s_{i}\Big)=s_{i}.

Then, as in the proof of Proposition
3.4
, the relations

s_{i}^{2}=1,\qquad s_{i}s_{i+1}s_{i}=s_{i+1}s_{i}s_{i+1},\qquad s_{j}s_{k}=s_{k}s_{j},

for
i,j,k\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
with
k\not\in\{j-1,j+1\}
,
imply the relations in (
3.3
).
Similarly, as in the proof of Proposition
3.4
, the relations

s_{i}X_{i}=X_{i+1}s_{i},\qquad s_{i}X_{i+1}=x_{i}s_{i},\qquad s_{i}X_{j}=X_{j}s_{i},

for
i\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
with
j\not\in\{i,i+1\}
, imply the relations in (
3.6
),
which, in the presence of the last relation in (
3.3
), are equivalent to the relations in (
3.4
).
∎

4.7.2
Proof of the Boson-Fermion equalities

The following result is a restatement of Theorem
4.4
.

Proposition 4.8
.

\begin{array}[]{llll}p_{0}\mathbb{C}[X]=\mathbb{C}[X]^{S_{n}}=\mathbb{C}[X]^{W_{0}},&e_{0}\mathbb{C}[X]=\mathbb{C}[X]^{\det}=a_{\rho}\mathbb{C}[X]^{W_{0}}&\hbox{and}&a_{\rho}=e_{0}x^{\rho},\\
\\
\mathbf{1}_{0}\mathbb{C}[X]=\mathbb{C}[X]^{\mathrm{Bos}}=\mathbb{C}[X]^{W_{0}},&\varepsilon_{0}\mathbb{C}[X]=\mathbb{C}[X]^{\mathrm{Fer}}=A_{\rho}\mathbb{C}[X]^{W_{0}}&\hbox{and}&A_{\rho}=\varepsilon_{0}x^{\rho}.\end{array}

Proof.

\circ

p_{0}\mathbb{C}[X]=\mathbb{C}[X]^{S_{n}}

–

If
f\in\mathbb{C}[X]^{S_{n}}
then
f=p_{0}\Big(\dfrac{1}{n!}f\Big)
so that
f\in p_{0}\mathbb{C}[X]
. Thus
\mathbb{C}[X]^{S_{n}}\subseteq p_{0}\mathbb{C}[X]
.

–

If
f\in p_{0}\mathbb{C}[X]
then
f=p_{0}g
. We also know that if
w\in S_{n}
then

wf=wp_{0}g=p_{0}g
, and so
f\in\mathbb{C}[X]^{S_{n}}
. Thus
p_{0}\mathbb{C}[X]\subseteq\mathbb{C}[X]^{S_{n}}
.

\circ

a_{\rho}\mathbb{C}[X]^{W_{0}}=\mathbb{C}[X]^{\det}

–

If
f\in\mathbb{C}[X]^{\det}
then
(1-s_{ij})f=0
, where
s_{ij}
denotes the transposition in
S_{n}
that switches
i
and
j
. Thus
f
is divisible by each
x_{j}-x_{i}
, which implies that

\hbox{$f$ is divisible by}\quad a_{\rho}=\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}x_{j}-x_{i}.

Then
\dfrac{1}{a_{\rho}}f\in\mathbb{C}[X]^{S_{n}}
and
f\in a_{\rho}\mathbb{C}[X]^{W_{0}}
, so that
\mathbb{C}[X]^{\det}\subseteq a_{\rho}\mathbb{C}[X]^{S_{n}}
.

\circ

e_{0}\mathbb{C}[X]=\mathbb{C}[X]^{\det}

–

If
f\in e_{0}\mathbb{C}[X]
then
s_{\alpha}f=s_{\alpha}e_{0}g=-e_{0}g=-f
. Thus
f\in\mathbb{C}[X]^{\det}
and
e_{0}\mathbb{C}[X]\subseteq\mathbb{C}[X]^{\det}
.

–

If
f\in\mathbb{C}[X]^{\det}
then
e_{0}f=\mathrm{Card}(W_{0})f
. Thus
f\in e_{0}\mathbb{C}[X]
and
\mathbb{C}[X]^{\det}\subseteq e_{0}\mathbb{C}[X]
.

\circ

\mathbf{1}_{0}\mathbb{C}[X]=\mathbb{C}[X]^{\mathrm{Bos}}=\mathbb{C}[X]^{S_{n}}

–

Let
h\in\mathbf{1_{0}}\mathbb{C}[X]
, we can write
h=\mathbf{1}_{0}f
with
f\in\mathbb{C}[X]
and then

T_{s_{i}}h=T_{s_{i}}\mathbf{1}_{0}f=t^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathbf{1}_{0}f=t^{\frac{1}{2}}h.\qquad\quad\hbox{Thus $h\in\mathbb{C}[X]^{\mathrm{Bos}}$ and $\mathbf{1}_{0}\mathbb{C}[X]\subseteq\mathbb{C}[X]^{\mathrm{Bos}}$.}

–

Let
f\in\mathbb{C}[X]^{\mathrm{Bos}}
. Then, by Proposition
3.6
and
(
4.7
),

f=\frac{t^{\frac{1}{2}\ell(w_{0}}}{W_{0}(t)}\mathbf{1}_{0}f=\frac{1}{[n]!}\sum_{w\in W_{0}}w\Big(f\ \prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\frac{x_{i}-tx_{j}}{x_{i}-x_{j}}\Big)\quad\in\mathbb{C}[X]^{W_{0}}.

Thus
\mathbb{C}[X]^{\mathrm{Bos}}\subseteq\mathbb{C}[X]^{W_{0}}
.

–

Let
f\in\mathbb{C}[X]^{W_{0}}
. Then, by Proposition
3.6
and
(
4.7
),

\mathbf{1}_{0}\frac{t^{\frac{1}{2}\ell(w_{0}}}{W_{0}(t)}f=\frac{1}{[n]!}\sum_{w\in W_{0}}w\Big(f\ \prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\frac{x_{i}-tx_{j}}{x_{i}-x_{j}}\Big)=f\frac{1}{[n]!}\sum_{w\in W_{0}}w\Big(\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\frac{x_{i}-tx_{j}}{x_{i}-x_{j}}\Big)=f.

Thus
f\in\mathbf{1}_{0}\mathbb{C}[X]
and
\mathbb{C}[X]^{W_{0}}\subseteq\mathbf{1}_{0}\mathbb{C}[X]
.

\circ

\varepsilon_{0}\mathbb{C}[X]=\mathbb{C}[X]^{\mathrm{Fer}}=A_{\rho}\mathbb{C}[X]^{W_{0}}

–

Let
h=\varepsilon_{0}\mathbb{C}[X]
and let
f\in\mathbb{C}[X]
such that
h=\varepsilon_{0}f
. Then

T_{s_{i}}h=T_{s_{i}}\varepsilon_{0}f=-t^{-\frac{1}{2}}\varepsilon_{0}f=-t^{-\frac{1}{2}}h.

Thus
h\in\mathbb{C}[X]^{\mathrm{Fer}}
and
\varepsilon_{0}\mathbb{C}[X]\subseteq\mathbb{C}[X]^{\mathrm{Fer}}
.

–

Let
f\in\mathbb{C}[X]^{\mathrm{Fer}}
.
Then
T_{i}f=-t^{-\frac{1}{2}}f
gives

f=\frac{t^{\frac{1}{2}\ell(w_{0})}}{W_{0}(t)}\varepsilon_{0}f=\frac{1}{W_{0}(t)}\frac{A_{\rho}}{a_{\rho}}\sum_{w\in W_{0}}\det(w)wf\quad\in A_{\rho}\mathbb{C}[X]^{W_{0}}.

Thus
\mathbb{C}[X]^{\mathrm{Fer}}\subseteq A_{\rho}\mathbb{C}[X]^{W_{0}}
.

–

Let
f\in A_{\rho}\mathbb{C}[X]^{W_{0}}
and
let
g\in\mathbb{C}[X]^{S_{n}}
be such that
f=A_{\rho}g
. Write
g
as a linear combination,
g=\sum c_{\lambda}s_{\lambda}
, where

s_{\lambda}
are Schur polynomials.
Then

f=A_{\rho}g=\sum_{\lambda}c_{\lambda}A_{\rho}s_{\lambda}=\sum_{\lambda}c_{\lambda}\frac{A_{\rho}}{a_{\rho}}\sum_{w\in W_{0}}\det(w_{0}w)wx^{\lambda+\rho}=\varepsilon_{0}\Big(\sum_{\lambda}c_{\lambda}x^{\lambda+\rho}\Big).

By (
3.20
) and the fact that

t^{-\frac{1}{2}\ell(w_{0})}\frac{A_{\rho}}{a_{\rho}}=t^{-\frac{1}{2}\ell(w_{0})}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\frac{x_{j}-tx_{i}}{x_{j}-x_{i}}=\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\frac{t^{-\frac{1}{2}}-t^{\frac{1}{2}}x_{i}x^{-1}_{j}}{1-x_{i}x^{-1}_{j}}=c_{w_{0}}(x)

conclude that
f\in\varepsilon_{0}\mathbb{C}[X]
. Thus
A_{\rho}\mathbb{C}[X]^{W_{0}}\subseteq\varepsilon_{0}\mathbb{C}[X]
.

∎

4.7.3
Proof of the formulas for Poincaré polynomials

Proposition
4.5
.

We have that

t^{-\frac{1}{2}\ell(w_{0})}W_{0}(t)=\mathrm{ev}^{t}_{0}(c_{w_{0}}^{Y})=\sum_{w\in S_{n}}wc_{w_{0}}^{X^{-1}}

and

W_{0}(t)=\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\frac{1-t^{j-i+1}}{1-t^{j-i}}=[n]!,\qquad\hbox{where}\qquad[n]!=\prod_{d=1}^{n}\frac{1-t^{d}}{1-t}.

Proof.

Identify
\mathbb{C}[X]
and
\widetilde{H}\mathbf{1}_{Y}
via the isomorphism in
(
4.3
).
Applying the identity
\mathbf{1}_{0}=p_{0}^{X}c_{w_{0}}^{X^{-1}}
from Proposition
3.20

to the polynomial
1=x^{0}=X^{0}\mathbf{1}_{Y}=\mathbf{1}_{Y}
,

\displaystyle t^{-\frac{1}{2}\ell(w_{0})}

\displaystyle\sum_{w\in S_{n}}w\Big(\prod_{i<j}\frac{x_{i}-tx_{j}}{x_{i}-x_{j}}\Big)=\Big(\sum_{w\in S_{n}}w\Big)c_{w_{0}}^{X^{-1}}\mathbf{1}_{Y}=p_{0}^{X}c^{X^{-1}}_{w_{0}}\mathbf{1}_{Y}=\mathbf{1}_{0}\mathbf{1}_{Y}\quad\hbox{and}

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{0}\mathbf{1}_{Y}

\displaystyle=\sum_{w\in S_{n}}(t^{\frac{1}{2}})^{\ell(w)-\ell(w_{0})}T_{w}\mathbf{1}_{Y}=t^{-\frac{1}{2}\ell(w_{0})}\Big(\sum_{w\in S_{n}}t^{\ell(w)}\Big)\mathbf{1}_{Y}=t^{-\frac{1}{2}\ell(w_{0})}W_{0}(t)\mathbf{1}_{Y}.

Applying the identity
\mathbf{1}_{0}=p_{0}^{Y}c^{Y}_{w_{0}}
from
Proposition
3.20
to
\mathbf{1}_{Y}
and using that
\eta_{w}\mathbf{1}_{Y}=0
if

w\in S_{n}
and
w\neq 1
(see the lest identity in (
3.13
)), gives

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{0}\mathbf{1}_{Y}

\displaystyle=p_{0}^{Y}c_{w_{0}}^{Y}=\Big(\sum_{w\in W_{0}}\eta_{w}\Big)c_{w_{0}}^{Y}\mathbf{1}_{Y}=\mathrm{ev}^{t}_{0}\Big(c_{w_{0}}^{Y}\Big)\Big(1+\sum_{w\in W_{0},w\neq 1}\eta_{w}\Big)\mathbf{1}_{Y}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{ev}^{t}_{0}\Big(c_{w_{0}}^{Y}\Big)\Big(1+0\Big)\mathbf{1}_{Y}=\mathrm{ev}^{t}_{0}\Big(c_{w_{0}}^{Y}\Big)\mathbf{1}_{Y}=\mathrm{ev}^{t}_{0}\Big(\prod_{i<j}\frac{t^{-\frac{1}{2}}-t^{\frac{1}{2}}Y_{i}Y^{-1}_{j}}{1-Y_{i}Y^{-1}_{j}}\Big)\mathbf{1}_{Y}

\displaystyle=\Big(\prod_{i<j}\frac{t^{-\frac{1}{2}}-t^{\frac{1}{2}}Y_{i}Y^{-1}_{j}}{1-Y_{i}Y^{-1}_{j}}\Big)\mathbf{1}_{Y}=t^{-\frac{1}{2}\ell(w_{0})}\Big(\prod_{i<j}\frac{1-t^{j-i+1}}{1-t^{j-i}}\Big)\mathbf{1}_{Y}.

Finally,

\displaystyle\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}

\displaystyle\frac{1-t^{j-i+1}}{1-t^{j-i}}=\prod_{d=1}^{n-1}\prod_{i<j\atop j-i=d}\frac{1-t^{d+1}}{1-t^{d}}=\prod_{d=1}^{n-1}\Big(\frac{1-t^{d+1}}{1-t^{d}}\Big)^{n-d}

\displaystyle=\Big(\frac{1-t^{n}}{1-t^{n-1}}\Big)\Big(\frac{1-t^{n-1}}{1-t^{n-2}}\Big)^{2}\cdots\Big(\frac{1-t^{2}}{1-t}\Big)^{n-1}=\frac{(1-t^{n})(1-t^{n-1})\cdots(1-t^{2})}{(1-t)^{n-1}}=[n]!.

∎

Proposition
4.6
.

Let
\lambda\in\left(\mathbb{Z}^{n}\right)^{+}
. Let

W_{\lambda}=\{v\in S_{n}\ |\ v\lambda=\lambda\}
be the stabilizer of
\lambda
in
S_{n}
and

W_{\lambda}(t)
is the length generating
function for
W_{\lambda}
. Then

t^{-\frac{1}{2}\ell(w_{\lambda})}W_{\lambda}(t)=\mathrm{ev}^{t}_{0}(c_{w_{\lambda}}^{Y})=\sum_{w\in W_{\lambda}}wc_{w_{\lambda}}^{X^{-1}},

where
w_{\lambda}
is the longest element of
W_{\lambda}
. Alternatively,

W_{\lambda}(t)=\sum_{w\in W_{\lambda}}w\Big(\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n\atop\lambda_{i}=\lambda_{j}}\frac{1-tx^{-1}_{i}x_{j}}{1-x^{-1}_{i}x_{j}}\Big)=\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n\atop\lambda_{i}=\lambda_{j}}\frac{1-t^{j-i+1}}{1-t^{j-i}}=\prod_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}[m_{i}]!,

where
m_{i}=\#\{k\in\{1,\ldots,n\}\ |\ \lambda_{k}=i\}
.

Proof.

Identify
\mathbb{C}[X]
and
\widetilde{H}\mathbf{1}_{Y}
via the isomorphism in
(
4.3
).
By the definition of
\mathbf{1}_{\lambda}
and
\mathbf{1}_{Y}
from (
3.23
) and (
4.2
),

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{\lambda}\mathbf{1}_{Y}

\displaystyle=\sum_{w\in W_{\lambda}}(t^{\frac{1}{2}})^{\ell(w)-\ell(w_{\lambda})}T_{w}\mathbf{1}_{Y}=t^{-\frac{1}{2}\ell(w_{\lambda})}\Big(\sum_{w\in W_{\lambda}}t^{\ell(w)}\Big)\mathbf{1}_{Y}=t^{-\frac{1}{2}\ell(w_{\lambda})}W_{\lambda}(t)\mathbf{1}_{Y}.

Let
v_{\lambda}=w_{0}w^{-1}_{\lambda}
so that
v_{\lambda}\in S_{n}
is minimal length
such that
v_{\lambda}\lambda
is weakly increasing.
Applying the identities
\mathbf{1}_{0}=p_{0}^{X}c_{w_{0}}^{X^{-1}}
and

\mathbf{1}_{0}=p_{\lambda}^{X}c_{v_{\lambda}}^{X^{-1}}\mathbf{1}_{\lambda}
from (
3.7
),
and using the facts that

\ell(v_{\lambda})+\ell(w_{\lambda})=\ell(w_{0})
and
the coefficient of
1
in
c_{v_{\lambda}}^{X^{-1}}
is
t^{-\frac{1}{2}\ell(v_{\lambda})}
, then
the expression

\displaystyle\Big(\sum_{w\in S_{n}}w\Big)c_{w_{0}}^{X^{-1}}E_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{1}_{0}E_{\lambda}\mathbf{1}_{Y}=p^{\lambda}_{X}c_{v_{\lambda}}^{X^{-1}}\mathbf{1}_{\lambda}E_{\lambda}\mathbf{1}_{Y}=p^{\lambda}_{X}c_{v_{\lambda}}^{X^{-1}}E_{\lambda}\mathbf{1}_{\lambda}\mathbf{1}_{Y}=t^{-\frac{1}{2}\ell(w_{\lambda})}W_{\lambda}(t)p^{\lambda}_{X}c_{v_{\lambda}}^{X^{-1}}E_{\lambda}\mathbf{1}_{Y}

has coefficient of
x^{\lambda}
equal to
t^{-\frac{1}{2}\ell(w_{0})}W_{\lambda}(t)
.
Since
\lambda_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{n}
and
the coefficient of
x^{\lambda}=x_{1}^{\lambda_{1}}\cdots x_{n}^{\lambda_{n}}
in
P_{\lambda}
is
1
then

P_{\lambda}=\frac{t^{\frac{1}{2}\ell(w_{0})}}{W_{\lambda}(t)}\Big(\sum_{w\in S_{n}}w\Big)c_{w_{0}}^{X^{-1}}E_{\lambda}=\frac{1}{W_{\lambda}(t)}\sum_{w\in S_{n}}w\Big(E_{\lambda}\prod_{i<j}\frac{x_{i}-tx_{j}}{x_{i}-x_{j}}\Big).

Applying the identity
\mathbf{1}_{\lambda}=p_{\lambda}^{Y}c_{w_{\lambda}}^{Y}
from Proposition (
3.7
)
to
\mathbf{1}_{Y}
and using that

\eta_{w}\mathbf{1}_{Y}=0
if
w\in S_{n}
and
w\neq 1
(see the last identity in (
3.13
)), gives

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{\lambda}\mathbf{1}_{Y}

\displaystyle=p_{\lambda}^{Y}c_{w_{\lambda}}^{Y}=\Big(\sum_{w\in W_{\lambda}}\eta_{w}\Big)c_{w_{\lambda}}^{Y}\mathbf{1}_{Y}=\mathrm{ev}_{0}^{t}\big(c_{w_{\lambda}}^{Y}\big)\Big(1+\sum_{w\in W_{\lambda},w\neq 1}\eta_{w}\Big)\mathbf{1}_{Y}=\mathrm{ev}_{0}^{t}\big(c_{w_{\lambda}}^{Y}\big)(1+0)\mathbf{1}_{Y}.

so that
t^{-\frac{1}{2}\ell(w_{\lambda})}W_{\lambda}(t)=\mathrm{ev}_{0}^{t}\big(c_{w_{\lambda}}^{Y}\big)
.
Explicitly,

\displaystyle\mathrm{ev}_{0}^{t}\big(c_{w_{\lambda}}^{Y}\big)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{ev}_{0}^{t}\Big(\prod_{i<j\atop\lambda_{i}=\lambda_{j}}\frac{t^{-\frac{1}{2}}-t^{\frac{1}{2}}Y_{i}Y^{-1}_{j}}{1-Y_{i}Y^{-1}_{j}}\Big)\mathbf{1}_{Y}=t^{-\frac{1}{2}\ell(w_{\lambda})}\Big(\prod_{i<j\atop\lambda_{i}=\lambda_{j}}\frac{1-t^{j-i+1}}{1-t^{j-i}}\Big)\mathbf{1}_{Y}.

Finally, letting
m_{i}=\#\{k\in\{1,\ldots,n\}\ |\ \lambda_{k}=i\}
,

\displaystyle\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n\atop\lambda_{i}=\lambda_{j}}\frac{1-t^{j-i+1}}{1-t^{j-i}}

\displaystyle=\prod_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\prod_{d_{i}=1}^{m_{i}-1}\prod_{i<j\atop j-i=d_{i}}\frac{1-t^{d_{i}+1}}{1-t^{d_{i}}}=\prod_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\prod_{d_{i}=1}^{m_{i}-1}\Big(\frac{1-t^{d_{i}+1}}{1-t^{d_{i}}}\Big)^{n-d_{i}}

\displaystyle=\prod_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\Big(\frac{1-t^{m_{i}}}{1-t^{m_{i}-1}}\Big)\Big(\frac{1-t^{m_{i}-1}}{1-t^{m_{i}-2}}\Big)^{2}\cdots\Big(\frac{1-t^{2}}{1-t}\Big)^{m_{i}-1}

\displaystyle=\prod_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{(1-t^{m_{i}})(1-t^{m_{i}-1})\cdots(1-t^{2})}{(1-t)^{{m_{i}}-1}}=\prod_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}[m_{i}]!.

∎

4.7.4
Proof of the eigenvalue formula

The following result establishes Theorem
1.1
.

Proposition 4.9
.

Let
\mu=(\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
. Then

Y_{i}E_{\mu}=q^{-\mu_{i}}t^{-(v_{\mu}(i)-1)+\frac{1}{2}(n-1)}E_{\mu}.

Proof.

First do the base case
\mu=0
when
E_{\mu}=1
.
Using
T_{\pi}1=1
and
T_{i}1=t^{\frac{1}{2}}1
and
T_{i}^{-1}1=t^{-\frac{1}{2}}1

gives

Y_{i}1=T^{-1}_{i-1}\cdots T^{-1}_{n-1}T_{\pi}T_{n-1}\cdots T_{i}\cdot 1=t^{\frac{1}{2}(-(i-1)+(n-i-1)}\cdot 1=t^{-(i-1)+\frac{1}{2}(n-1)}\cdot 1.

Then, by the creation formula for
E_{\mu}
in Theorem
4.2
and the relations (
3.11
) and (
3.12
) for moving
Y_{i}
past
\tau^{\vee}_{j}
,

\displaystyle Y_{i}E_{\mu}

\displaystyle=t^{-\frac{1}{2}\ell(v_{\mu}^{-1})}\tau^{\vee}_{u_{\mu}}1=t^{-\frac{1}{2}\ell(v_{\mu}^{-1})}\tau^{\vee}_{u_{\mu}}Y_{u^{-1}_{\mu}(i)}1=t^{-\frac{1}{2}\ell(v_{\mu}^{-1})}\tau^{\vee}_{u_{\mu}}q^{-\mu_{i}}Y_{v_{\mu}(i)})1

\displaystyle=t^{-\frac{1}{2}\ell(v_{\mu}^{-1})}\tau^{\vee}_{u_{\mu}}q^{-\mu_{i}}t^{-(v_{\mu}(i)-1)+\frac{1}{2}(n-1)}1=q^{-\mu_{i}}t^{-(v_{\mu}(i)-1)+\frac{1}{2}(n-1)}E_{\mu}.

∎

4.7.5
Proof of the E-expansions

Proposition
4.7
.

Let
\lambda\in\left(\mathbb{Z}^{n}\right)^{+}
. Then

\displaystyle P_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\sum_{z\in W^{\lambda}}t^{\frac{1}{2}\ell(v_{\lambda}z)}\mathrm{ev}^{t}_{z\lambda}(c^{Y}_{v_{\lambda}z})E_{z\lambda}\qquad\hbox{and}

\displaystyle A_{\lambda+\rho}

\displaystyle=\sum_{z\in W_{0}}(-t^{\frac{1}{2}})^{\ell(w_{0}z)}\mathrm{ev}^{t}_{z(\lambda+\rho)}(c^{Y^{-1}}_{w_{0}z})E_{z(\lambda+\rho)}.

Alternatively,

\displaystyle P_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mu\in S_{n}\lambda}\Big(\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n\atop\mu_{i}>\mu_{j}}t\Big(\frac{1-q^{\mu_{i}-\mu_{j}}t^{v_{\mu}(i)-v_{\mu}(j)-1}}{1-q^{\mu_{i}-\mu_{j}}t^{v_{\mu}(i)-v_{\mu}(j)}}\Big)\Big)E_{\mu}\qquad\hbox{and}

\displaystyle A_{\lambda+\rho}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mu\in S_{n}(\lambda+\rho)}\Big(\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n\atop\mu_{i}>\mu_{j}}(-1)\Big(\frac{1-q^{\mu_{i}-\mu_{j}}t^{v_{\mu}(i)-v_{\mu}(j)+1}}{1-q^{\mu_{i}-\mu_{j}}t^{v_{\mu}(i)-v_{\mu}(j)}}\Big)\Big)E_{\mu}.

Proof.

Note that the coefficient of
E_{w_{0}\lambda}
in
P_{\lambda}
is 1 and the coefficient
of
E_{w_{0}(\lambda+\rho)}
in
A_{\lambda+\rho}
is 1.

For the first statement, (
3.20
), (
4.4
) and (
4.7
) give

\displaystyle P_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\frac{t^{\frac{1}{2}\ell(w_{0})}}{W_{\lambda}(t)}\mathbf{1}_{0}E_{\lambda}=\frac{t^{\frac{1}{2}(\ell(w_{0})-\ell(w_{\lambda}))}}{t^{-\frac{1}{2}\ell(w_{\lambda})}W_{\lambda}(t)}\Big(\sum_{z\in W^{\lambda}}\eta_{z}\Big)c_{v_{\lambda}}^{Y}\mathbf{1}_{\lambda}E_{\lambda}=t^{\frac{1}{2}(\ell(w_{0})-\ell(w_{\lambda}))}\sum_{z\in W^{\lambda}}\tau^{\vee}_{z}\frac{c_{v_{\lambda}}^{Y}}{c_{z}^{Y}}E_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=t^{\frac{1}{2}\ell(v_{\lambda})}\sum_{z\in W^{\lambda}}\tau^{\vee}_{z}(z^{-1}c_{v_{\lambda}z}^{Y})E_{\lambda}=t^{\frac{1}{2}\ell(v_{\lambda})}\sum_{z\in W^{\lambda}}c_{v_{\lambda}z}^{Y}\tau^{\vee}_{z}E_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=t^{\frac{1}{2}\ell(v_{\lambda})}\sum_{z\in W^{\lambda}}\mathrm{ev}^{t}_{z\lambda}(c_{v_{\lambda}z}^{Y})t^{-\frac{1}{2}\ell(z)}E_{z\lambda}=\sum_{z\in W^{\lambda}}t^{\frac{1}{2}\ell(v_{\lambda}z)}\mathrm{ev}^{t}_{z\lambda}(c_{v_{\lambda}z}^{Y})E_{z\lambda}.

If
z\in W^{\lambda}
and
\mu=z\lambda
then

\mathrm{Inv}(v_{\lambda}z)=\{(i,j)\ |\ \hbox{$1\leq i<j\leq n$ and $\mu_{i}>\mu_{j}$}\}
,
so that

\displaystyle t^{\frac{1}{2}\ell(v_{\lambda}z)}\mathrm{ev}^{t}_{\mu}(c_{v_{\lambda}z}^{Y})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{ev}^{t}_{\mu}\Big(\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n\atop\mu_{i}>\mu_{j}}t^{\frac{1}{2}}\frac{t^{-\frac{1}{2}}-t^{\frac{1}{2}}Y_{i}Y^{-1}_{j}}{1-Y_{i}Y^{-1}_{j}}\Big)=\mathrm{ev}^{t}_{\mu}\Big(\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n\atop\mu_{i}>\mu_{j}}t\frac{t^{-1}Y^{-1}_{i}Y_{j}-1}{Y^{-1}_{i}Y_{j}-1}\Big)

\displaystyle=\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n\atop\mu_{i}>\mu_{j}}t\Big(\frac{1-q^{\mu_{i}-\mu_{j}}t^{v_{\mu}(i)-v_{\mu}(j)-1}}{1-q^{\mu_{i}-\mu_{j}}t^{v_{\mu}(i)-v_{\mu}(j)}}\Big),

(4.11)

where we have used that

\mathrm{ev}^{t}_{\mu}(Y_{i})=q^{-\mu_{i}}t^{-(v_{\mu}(i)-1)+\frac{1}{2}(n-1)}
. Thus

\mathrm{ev}^{t}_{\mu}(Y^{-1}_{i}Y_{j})=q^{\mu_{i}}t^{(v_{\mu}(i)-1)-\frac{1}{2}(n-1)}q^{-\mu_{j}}t^{-(v_{\mu}(j)-1)+\frac{1}{2}(n-1)}=q^{\mu_{i}-\mu_{j}}t^{v_{\mu}(i)-v_{\mu}(j)}.

For the second statement,

\displaystyle A_{\lambda+\rho}(q,t)

\displaystyle=t^{\frac{1}{2}\ell(w_{0})}\varepsilon_{0}E_{\lambda+\rho}(q,t)=t^{\frac{1}{2}\ell(w_{0})}c_{w_{0}}^{Y^{-1}}\sum_{z\in S_{n}}\det(w_{0}z)\eta_{z}E_{\lambda+\rho}(q,t)

\displaystyle=t^{\frac{1}{2}\ell(w_{0})}\sum_{z\in S_{n}}\det(w_{0}z)c_{w_{0}z}^{Y^{-1}}t^{-\frac{1}{2}\ell(z)}t^{\frac{1}{2}\ell(z)}\tau^{\vee}_{z}E_{\lambda+\rho}(q,t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{z\in S_{n}}\det(w_{0}z)c_{w_{0}z}^{Y^{-1}}t^{\frac{1}{2}\ell(w_{0}z)}E_{z(\lambda+\rho)}(q,t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{z\in S_{n}}(-1)^{\ell(w_{0}z)}t^{\frac{1}{2}\ell(w_{0}z)}\mathrm{ev}^{t}_{z(\lambda+\rho)}(c_{w_{0}z}^{Y^{-1}})E_{z(\lambda+\rho)}(q,t).

If
\mu=z(\lambda+\rho)
then, in a manner similar to the computation in (
4.11
),

\displaystyle(-t^{\frac{1}{2}})^{\ell(w_{0}z)}\mathrm{ev}^{t}_{\mu}(c_{w_{0}z}^{Y^{-1}})

\displaystyle=\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n\atop\mu_{i}>\mu_{j}}(-t^{\frac{1}{2}})\frac{t^{-\frac{1}{2}}-t^{\frac{1}{2}}Y^{-1}_{i}Y_{j}}{1-Y^{-1}_{i}Y_{j}}

\displaystyle=\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n\atop\mu_{i}>\mu_{j}}(-1)\frac{1-tq^{\mu_{i}-\mu_{j}}t^{v_{\mu}(i)-v_{\mu}(j)\rangle}}{1-q^{\mu_{i}-\mu_{j}}t^{v_{\mu}(i)-v_{\mu}(j)\rangle}}.

(4.12)

∎

5
Principal specializations and hook formulas

In Section
4
we saw that
c
-functions provide the explicit constants for normalization
and the
E
-expansion of bosonic and fermionic Macdonald polynomials. In this section we see the role
of
c
-functions in the amazing product formulas for principal specializations.
These principal specializations capture many of
the hook type formulas that appear in formulas for dimensions of irreducible representations
of the general linear group and the symmetric group. As discussed in Section
1.2
,
the representation theoretic interpretation of the hook formulas that arise from the principal specializations
of Macdonald polynomials is still rather mysterious, but we hope
that viewing these results as being sourced from evaluations of
c
-functions will help to provide insight.
Our exposition follows
[
Mac03
, (5.2.14) and (5.3.9)]
(with some streamlining) for the
c
-function formulas,
and then follows
[
AGY22
]
for rewriting the
c
-function formulas into hook formulas.
This provides an alternative route to the proof of the principal specialization formulas for
P_{\lambda}

which are given in
[
Mac
, (
6.11^{\prime}
)]
.

5.1
c
-function formulas

An
n
-periodic permutation
is a bijection
w\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}
such that
w(i+n)=w(i)+n
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Given an
n
-periodic permutation
w
, define its
set of inversions
and
length
by

\mathrm{Inv}(w)=\left\{(i,k)\ \Big|\ \begin{array}[]{c}\hbox{$i\in\{1,\ldots,n\},k\in\mathbb{Z}$}\\
\hbox{$i<k$ and $w(i)>w(k)$}\end{array}\right\}\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad\ell(w)=\#\mathrm{Inv}(w).

Define an action of the
n
-periodic permutations on
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
by setting

w(\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{n})=(\mu_{v(1)}+\ell_{1},\ldots,\mu_{v(n)}+\ell_{n}),

where
v(i)\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
and
\ell_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
are determined by

w(i)=v(i)+\ell_{i}n
.
Given
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
, we consider the following
n
-periodic permutations associated to
\mu

\begin{array}[]{l}\hbox{$u_{\mu}$ be the minimal length $n$-periodic permutation such that
$u_{\mu}(0,\ldots,0)=(\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{n})$ and }\\
\hbox{$t_{\mu}$ be the $n$-periodic permutation given by $t_{\mu}(i)=i+n\mu_{i}$.}\end{array}

Recall that
v_{\mu}\in S_{n}
denotes the minimal length permutation such that
v_{\mu}\mu
is weakly increasing. The three permutations are related by
u_{\mu}=t_{\mu}v_{\mu}^{-1}
.

We extend the definition of
c
-functions to
n
-periodic permutations.
For
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
and
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}
define

c^{Y^{-1}}_{(i,j+\ell n)}=t^{-\frac{1}{2}}\frac{1-q^{\ell}tY_{i}^{-1}Y_{j}}{1-q^{\ell}Y_{i}^{-1}Y_{j}}\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad c^{Y^{-1}}_{w}=\prod_{(i,k)\in\mathrm{Inv}(w)}c^{Y^{-1}}_{(i,k)}.

Define ring homomorphisms
\mathrm{ev}^{t}_{0}\colon\mathbb{C}[Y]\to\mathbb{C}
and
\mathrm{ev}^{t^{-1}}_{0}\colon\mathbb{C}[Y]\to\mathbb{C}

by

\mathrm{ev}^{t}_{0}(Y_{i})=t^{-(i-1)+\frac{1}{2}(n-1)}\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad\mathrm{ev}^{t^{-1}}_{0}(Y_{i})=t^{(i-1)-\frac{1}{2}(n-1)},\qquad\hbox{for $i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}$.}

Theorem 5.1
.

Let
\mu,\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
with
\lambda_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{n}
.
Then

\displaystyle E_{\mu}(1,t,t^{2},\ldots,t^{n-1};q,t)

\displaystyle=t^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}|\lambda|}t^{-\frac{1}{2}\ell(v^{-1}_{\mu})}\mathrm{ev}^{t}_{0}(c^{Y^{-1}}_{u_{\mu}}),

\displaystyle P_{\lambda}(1,t,t^{2},\ldots,t^{n-1};q,t)

\displaystyle=t^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}|\lambda|}\mathrm{ev}^{t^{-1}}_{0}(c^{Y^{-1}}_{t_{\lambda}})\qquad\hbox{and}

\displaystyle A_{\lambda+\rho}(1,t,t^{2},\ldots,t^{n-1};q,t)

\displaystyle=0.

Let
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}_{\geq 0}
.
Using the formulas (see
[
GR21
, Proposition 2.1(d) and Proposition 2.2(b)]
)

\mathrm{Inv}(t_{\lambda})=\{(i,j+\ell n)\ |\ \hbox{$i,j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}$, $i<j$ and $\ell\in\{0,1,\ldots,\lambda_{j}-\lambda_{i}-1\}$}\}

(5.1)

and

\mathrm{Inv}(u_{\mu})=\{(v_{\mu}(r),i+(\mu_{r}-c+1)n)\ |\ \hbox{$(r,c)\in\mu$ and $i\in\{1,\ldots,u_{\mu}(r,c)\}$}\}.

(5.2)

gives the following corollary.

Corollary 5.2
.

Let
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}_{\geq 0}
. Denote by
\lambda
the decreasing rearrangement of
\mu
and
n(\lambda)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(i-1)\lambda_{i}
.
Then

P_{\lambda}(1,t,t^{2},\ldots,t^{n-1};q,t)=t^{n(\lambda)}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\prod_{\ell=0}^{\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j}-1}\frac{1-q^{\ell}t^{j-i+1}}{1-q^{\ell}t^{j-i}}

and

E_{\mu}(1,t,t^{2},\ldots,t^{n-1};q,t)=t^{-\frac{1}{2}\ell(v^{-1}_{\mu})}\prod_{(r,c)\in\mu}\prod_{i=1}^{u_{\mu}(r,c)}\frac{1-q^{\mu_{r}-c+1}t^{v_{\mu}(r)-i+1}}{1-q^{\mu_{r}-c+1}t^{v_{\mu}(r)-i}}.

5.2
Hook formulas for the bosonic and electronic cases

Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}_{\geq 0}
with

\lambda_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{n}
.
Let
\lambda^{\prime}
denote the conjugate partition to
\lambda
(i.e. for
c\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
let

\lambda^{\prime}_{c}=\#\{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\ |\ \lambda_{j}\geq c\}
). A
box in
\lambda
is a
pair
b=(r,c)
with
r\in\{1,\dots,n\}
and
c\in\{1,\ldots,\lambda_{i}\}
.
For a box
b=(r,c)
in
\lambda
define

\begin{matrix}\hbox to142.67pt{\vbox to142.67pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-142.46379pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{68.98607pt}{-46.15135pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{73}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
